People keep saying I’m doing things wrong, but the boxes have a kind of order if you look at them right, like little roads, you know? \n\nSometimes I’ll just stop by the forklift with my arms up, and they yell because it’s in the way, and I copy the beeping sounds or the hand signals the guys do. It’s not really… I don’t know, it just happens. They get mad and tell me to stop moving boxes into patterns when I’m supposed to be loading them.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the patterns you see in the boxes are real, or are they just something you imagine?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, the boxes are real, obviously, you can touch them, and the tape lines and labels, they line up sometimes. But if you mean like, does everybody see it the way I do, I don’t know. \n\nAt the warehouse they just want them stacked fast, like blue pallet over here, damaged ones over there, and I try to explain it’s cleaner if the corners match, like when you’re lining up notebooks for class— I used to do that, keep the edges straight. So… I’m not sure if it’s imagined. It feels like it’s there when I’m looking at it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear voices or sounds when no one else is around?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… sounds, yeah, there’s always sounds, like the building makes noises even when it’s quiet, the lights buzz and the carts kind of squeak in my head after work. Voices though… I mean, if someone’s not there? I don’t really know how you’d tell for sure.\n\nSometimes I think I hear my name, but then it could be the hallway or somebody outside, or like when the forklift backs up and the beep gets stuck in your ears. My old dorm had pipes that sounded like people talking, too, especially at night. So… I haven’t really noticed it in a clear way, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the patterns you see in the boxes have a special meaning or purpose?\n\nPatient: Special meaning… I don’t know if I’d call it special. It’s more like… if the boxes line up wrong, it feels messy, like when a song is off-beat. But purpose, I mean, everything in the warehouse has a purpose, right? Labels, numbers, where stuff goes.\n\nSometimes I think if I put them the right way it makes more sense, but I don’t know that it means anything beyond that. My old notebooks used to be like that too, all the corners even, and if someone borrowed one and bent the pages I’d keep thinking about it. So… I’m not sure. It just feels like there’s a right way they should be.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Controlling… I don’t know. I don’t think of it like someone with a remote or anything. I’m just trying to get them right, like the edges and the labels, because if one is turned weird it kind of pulls my attention.

But “something” controlling it… I mean, work kind of controls it too, because the scanner tells you where things go, and the supervisor wants it fast, and then you start moving without thinking. Like when I used to walk to class and suddenly you’re there and you don’t remember every step. So… I’m not sure that’s what you mean. I haven’t really noticed someone making me do it.

Yeah… I guess it is. I mean, I didn’t think it was like a “problem” problem at first, just me trying to do the job right, but then they took me off the forklift. People keep saying I’m doing things wrong, but the boxes have a kind of order if you look at them right, like little roads, you know? \n\nSometimes I’ll just stop by the forklift with my arms up, and they yell because it’s in the way, and I copy the beeping sounds or the hand signals the guys do. It’s not really… I don’t know, it just happens. They get mad and tell me to stop moving boxes into patterns when I’m supposed to be loading them.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the patterns you see in the boxes are real, or are they just something you imagine?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, the boxes are real, obviously, you can touch them, and the tape lines and labels, they line up sometimes. But if you mean like, does everybody see it the way I do, I don’t know. \n\nAt the warehouse they just want them stacked fast, like blue pallet over here, damaged ones over there, and I try to explain it’s cleaner if the corners match, like when you’re lining up notebooks for class— I used to do that, keep the edges straight. So… I’m not sure if it’s imagined. It feels like it’s there when I’m looking at it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear voices or sounds when no one else is around?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… sounds, yeah, there’s always sounds, like the building makes noises even when it’s quiet, the lights buzz and the carts kind of squeak in my head after work. Voices though… I mean, if someone’s not there? I don’t really know how you’d tell for sure.\n\nSometimes I think I hear my name, but then it could be the hallway or somebody outside, or like when the forklift backs up and the beep gets stuck in your ears. My old dorm had pipes that sounded like people talking, too, especially at night. So… I haven’t really noticed it in a clear way, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the patterns you see in the boxes have a special meaning or purpose?\n\nPatient: Special meaning… I don’t know if I’d call it special. It’s more like… if the boxes line up wrong, it feels messy, like when a song is off-beat. But purpose, I mean, everything in the warehouse has a purpose, right? Labels, numbers, where stuff goes.\n\nSometimes I think if I put them the right way it makes more sense, but I don’t know that it means anything beyond that. My old notebooks used to be like that too, all the corners even, and if someone borrowed one and bent the pages I’d keep thinking about it. So… I’m not sure. It just feels like there’s a right way they should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you think someone or something is controlling your actions when you arrange the boxes into patterns?\n\nPatient: Controlling… I don’t know. I don’t think of it like someone with a remote or anything. I’m just trying to get them right, like the edges and the labels, because if one is turned weird it kind of pulls my attention.\n\nBut “something” controlling it… I mean, work kind of controls it too, because the scanner tells you where things go, and the supervisor wants it fast, and then you start moving without thinking. Like when I used to walk to class and suddenly you’re there and you don’t remember every step. So… I’m not sure that’s what you mean. I haven’t really noticed someone making me do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you can't stop thinking about arranging the boxes into patterns, even when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know. Sometimes after work I still see the stacks in my head, like when you’ve been staring at something too long and then you close your eyes and it’s still kind of there. But I don’t know if that’s the same as not being able to stop thinking about it.\n\nLike, when I was in school, after doing math homework, I’d keep seeing the numbers for a while, or if a song is playing at the store it gets stuck in your head even if you don’t like it. The boxes are kind of like that maybe. I haven’t really… noticed it in a way where I’d say it takes over or something. It’s just there sometimes, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your way of arranging the boxes or thinking about patterns is causing problems for you in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it is. I mean, I didn’t think it was like a “problem” problem at first, just me trying to do the job right, but then they took me off the forklift. They said it wasn’t safe, or that I was getting too distracted with where things were set, and… I don’t know, I used to be good with the forklift.\n\nAnd school too, I’m behind in my community college classes. I’ll sit down to do the work and then I start thinking about rows and labels and how things should line up, and then it’s like the whole evening is gone. It reminds me of when my uncle would organize his garage by color, but he could still find everything, so I don’t know why mine turns into such a mess.\n\nI also stopped going to the church food-bank shifts. People there were saying they couldn’t count on me, or they didn’t feel like things were safe around me anymore. That one kind of… yeah, that bothered me. I liked helping there. So I guess it’s causing problems, even if I don’t always see it happening right away.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t sit there and think, “I don’t want to think about boxes,” exactly. It’s more like my mind goes there if something reminds me, like if I see shelves at a store or the way cereal boxes are lined up, and then I start noticing which one is crooked.

But I don’t know if that counts as even when I don’t want to. Sometimes I’m trying to do school stuff and it pops in, but then I also end up thinking about other things too, like whether I paid my phone bill or what time the bus comes. The bus route near my apartment changed and now the timing is all off, so I have to leave earlier, and that kind of messes up the whole day.

So… yeah, I guess it happens when I’m not trying to think about it, but I don’t know if I’d say I “don’t want to.” It’s just there sometimes.

It’s just there sometimes, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your way of arranging the boxes or thinking about patterns is causing problems for you in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it is. I mean, I didn’t think it was like a “problem” problem at first, just me trying to do the job right, but then they took me off the forklift. They said it wasn’t safe, or that I was getting too distracted with where things were set, and… I don’t know, I used to be good with the forklift.\n\nAnd school too, I’m behind in my community college classes. I’ll sit down to do the work and then I start thinking about rows and labels and how things should line up, and then it’s like the whole evening is gone. It reminds me of when my uncle would organize his garage by color, but he could still find everything, so I don’t know why mine turns into such a mess.\n\nI also stopped going to the church food-bank shifts. People there were saying they couldn’t count on me, or they didn’t feel like things were safe around me anymore. That one kind of… yeah, that bothered me. I liked helping there. So I guess it’s causing problems, even if I don’t always see it happening right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're thinking about arranging the boxes into patterns even when you don't want to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t sit there and think, “I don’t want to think about boxes,” exactly. It’s more like my mind goes there if something reminds me, like if I see shelves at a store or the way cereal boxes are lined up, and then I start noticing which one is crooked.\n\nBut I don’t know if that counts as even when I don’t want to. The bus route near my apartment changed and now the timing is all off, so I have to leave earlier, and that kind of messes up the whole day.\n\nSo… yeah, I guess it happens when I’m not trying to think about it, but I don’t know if I’d say I “don’t want to.” It’s just there sometimes.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I don’t know if I’d say “unable,” exactly. Like, sometimes I try to read for class and then I realize I’m thinking about how the page is laid out, or how the boxes at work would fit better if they were turned the other way, but then… I don’t know, everybody’s mind wanders, right?

It’s kind of like when my roommate keeps the dishes in the wrong cabinet and then I’ll think about that while I’m supposed to be doing something else. But then I’ll also think about the bus schedule, or whether I left my work gloves in my locker, or that time the labels got put on the wrong pallets and nobody noticed until the end of the shift.

So… I guess I notice the patterns come up when I’m trying to focus, but I’m not sure if that means I can’t stop. I haven’t really timed it or anything.

Sometimes I’m trying to do school stuff and it pops in, but then I also end up thinking about other things too, like whether I paid my phone bill or what time the bus comes. The bus route near my apartment changed and now the timing is all off, so I have to leave earlier, and that kind of messes up the whole day.\n\nSo… yeah, I guess it happens when I’m not trying to think about it, but I don’t know if I’d say I “don’t want to.” It’s just there sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop thinking about the patterns, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “unable,” exactly. Like, sometimes I try to read for class and then I realize I’m thinking about how the page is laid out, or how the boxes at work would fit better if they were turned the other way, but then… I don’t know, everybody’s mind wanders, right?\n\nIt’s kind of like when my roommate keeps the dishes in the wrong cabinet and then I’ll think about that while I’m supposed to be doing something else. Yeah… I mean, it feels like I’m supposed to put them a certain way, even if nobody wrote it on the sheet or anything. But then it gets weird because I’ll start making these little patterns with the boxes, like turning them or setting them in rows that make sense to me, and people are yelling, “Stop, that’s not where that goes.”

And sometimes I just kind of freeze there by the forklift with my arms up, like I’m halfway through doing the hand signal, and then I hear the backup beeper and I’ll copy it without really meaning to. That’s what they keep bringing up, I think. My supervisor said it wasn’t even about the boxes being required anymore, it was me doing strange stuff when everyone needed me to move out of the way.

It reminds me of those crosswalk signals that beep different in different neighborhoods—sorry, I know that’s not what you asked. But yeah, I guess I do get stuck on doing it a specific way, even when it’s not part of the job.

I mean, I didn’t think it was like a “problem” problem at first, just me trying to do the job right, but then they took me off the forklift. They said it wasn’t safe, or that I was getting too distracted with where things were set, and… I don’t know, I used to be good with the forklift.\n\nAnd school too, I’m behind in my community college classes. I’ll sit down to do the work and then I start thinking about rows and labels and how things should line up, and then it’s like the whole evening is gone. It reminds me of when my uncle would organize his garage by color, but he could still find everything, so I don’t know why mine turns into such a mess.\n\nI also stopped going to the church food-bank shifts. People there were saying they couldn’t count on me, or they didn’t feel like things were safe around me anymore. That one kind of… yeah, that bothered me. I liked helping there. So I guess it’s causing problems, even if I don’t always see it happening right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're thinking about arranging the boxes into patterns even when you don't want to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t sit there and think, “I don’t want to think about boxes,” exactly. It’s more like my mind goes there if something reminds me, like if I see shelves at a store or the way cereal boxes are lined up, and then I start noticing which one is crooked.\n\nBut I don’t know if that counts as even when I don’t want to. Sometimes I’m trying to do school stuff and it pops in, but then I also end up thinking about other things too, like whether I paid my phone bill or what time the bus comes. The bus route near my apartment changed and now the timing is all off, so I have to leave earlier, and that kind of messes up the whole day.\n\nSo… yeah, I guess it happens when I’m not trying to think about it, but I don’t know if I’d say I “don’t want to.” It’s just there sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop thinking about the patterns, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “unable,” exactly. Like, sometimes I try to read for class and then I realize I’m thinking about how the page is laid out, or how the boxes at work would fit better if they were turned the other way, but then… I don’t know, everybody’s mind wanders, right?\n\nIt’s kind of like when my roommate keeps the dishes in the wrong cabinet and then I’ll think about that while I’m supposed to be doing something else. But then I’ll also think about the bus schedule, or whether I left my work gloves in my locker, or that time the labels got put on the wrong pallets and nobody noticed until the end of the shift.\n\nSo… I guess I notice the patterns come up when I’m trying to focus, but I’m not sure if that means I can’t stop. I haven’t really timed it or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong need to arrange the boxes in a specific way, even if it's not required for your job?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it feels like I’m supposed to put them a certain way, even if nobody wrote it on the sheet or anything. But then it gets weird because I’ll start making these little patterns with the boxes, like turning them or setting them in rows that make sense to me, and people are yelling, “Stop, that’s not where that goes.”\n\nAnd sometimes I just kind of freeze there by the forklift with my arms up, like I’m halfway through doing the hand signal, and then I hear the backup beeper and I’ll copy it without really meaning to. That’s what they keep bringing up, I think. My supervisor said it wasn’t even about the boxes being required anymore, it was me doing strange stuff when everyone needed me to move out of the way.\n\nIt reminds me of those crosswalk signals that beep different in different neighborhoods—sorry, I know that’s not what you asked. But yeah, I guess I do get stuck on doing it a specific way, even when it’s not part of the job.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yeah, sort of… I mean, I don’t count like “one, two, three” or anything. But at work they say I keep doing the same kinds of things again, like I’ll get stuck with my arms up by the forklift, like I’m frozen in the middle of a hand signal, and then if the beeper goes off I might copy the sound without really thinking.

The forklift beeping is really loud in that warehouse, though, and it kind of gets in your head. But if you mean like, does everybody see it the way I do, I don’t know. \n\nAt the warehouse they just want them stacked fast, like blue pallet over here, damaged ones over there, and I try to explain it’s cleaner if the corners match, like when you’re lining up notebooks for class— I used to do that, keep the edges straight. So… I’m not sure if it’s imagined. It feels like it’s there when I’m looking at it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear voices or sounds when no one else is around?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… sounds, yeah, there’s always sounds, like the building makes noises even when it’s quiet, the lights buzz and the carts kind of squeak in my head after work. Voices though… I mean, if someone’s not there? I don’t really know how you’d tell for sure.\n\nSometimes I think I hear my name, but then it could be the hallway or somebody outside, or like when the forklift backs up and the beep gets stuck in your ears. My old dorm had pipes that sounded like people talking, too, especially at night. So… I haven’t really noticed it in a clear way, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the patterns you see in the boxes have a special meaning or purpose?\n\nPatient: Special meaning… I don’t know if I’d call it special. It’s more like… if the boxes line up wrong, it feels messy, like when a song is off-beat. But purpose, I mean, everything in the warehouse has a purpose, right? Labels, numbers, where stuff goes.\n\nSometimes I think if I put them the right way it makes more sense, but I don’t know that it means anything beyond that. My old notebooks used to be like that too, all the corners even, and if someone borrowed one and bent the pages I’d keep thinking about it. So… I’m not sure. It just feels like there’s a right way they should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you think someone or something is controlling your actions when you arrange the boxes into patterns?\n\nPatient: Controlling… I don’t know. I don’t think of it like someone with a remote or anything. I’m just trying to get them right, like the edges and the labels, because if one is turned weird it kind of pulls my attention.\n\nBut “something” controlling it… I mean, work kind of controls it too, because the scanner tells you where things go, and the supervisor wants it fast, and then you start moving without thinking. Like when I used to walk to class and suddenly you’re there and you don’t remember every step. So… I’m not sure that’s what you mean. I haven’t really noticed someone making me do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you can't stop thinking about arranging the boxes into patterns, even when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know. Sometimes after work I still see the stacks in my head, like when you’ve been staring at something too long and then you close your eyes and it’s still kind of there. But I don’t know if that’s the same as not being able to stop thinking about it.\n\nLike, when I was in school, after doing math homework, I’d keep seeing the numbers for a while, or if a song is playing at the store it gets stuck in your head even if you don’t like it. The boxes are kind of like that maybe. I haven’t really… noticed it in a way where I’d say it takes over or something. It’s just there sometimes, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your way of arranging the boxes or thinking about patterns is causing problems for you in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it is. I mean, I didn’t think it was like a “problem” problem at first, just me trying to do the job right, but then they took me off the forklift. They said it wasn’t safe, or that I was getting too distracted with where things were set, and… I don’t know, I used to be good with the forklift.\n\nAnd school too, I’m behind in my community college classes. I’ll sit down to do the work and then I start thinking about rows and labels and how things should line up, and then it’s like the whole evening is gone. It reminds me of when my uncle would organize his garage by color, but he could still find everything, so I don’t know why mine turns into such a mess.\n\nI also stopped going to the church food-bank shifts. People there were saying they couldn’t count on me, or they didn’t feel like things were safe around me anymore. That one kind of… yeah, that bothered me. I liked helping there. So I guess it’s causing problems, even if I don’t always see it happening right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're thinking about arranging the boxes into patterns even when you don't want to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t sit there and think, “I don’t want to think about boxes,” exactly. It’s more like my mind goes there if something reminds me, like if I see shelves at a store or the way cereal boxes are lined up, and then I start noticing which one is crooked.\n\nBut I don’t know if that counts as even when I don’t want to. Sometimes I’m trying to do school stuff and it pops in, but then I also end up thinking about other things too, like whether I paid my phone bill or what time the bus comes. The bus route near my apartment changed and now the timing is all off, so I have to leave earlier, and that kind of messes up the whole day.\n\nSo… yeah, I guess it happens when I’m not trying to think about it, but I don’t know if I’d say I “don’t want to.” It’s just there sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop thinking about the patterns, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “unable,” exactly. Like, sometimes I try to read for class and then I realize I’m thinking about how the page is laid out, or how the boxes at work would fit better if they were turned the other way, but then… I don’t know, everybody’s mind wanders, right?\n\nIt’s kind of like when my roommate keeps the dishes in the wrong cabinet and then I’ll think about that while I’m supposed to be doing something else. But then I’ll also think about the bus schedule, or whether I left my work gloves in my locker, or that time the labels got put on the wrong pallets and nobody noticed until the end of the shift.\n\nSo… I guess I notice the patterns come up when I’m trying to focus, but I’m not sure if that means I can’t stop. I haven’t really timed it or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong need to arrange the boxes in a specific way, even if it's not required for your job?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it feels like I’m supposed to put them a certain way, even if nobody wrote it on the sheet or anything. But then it gets weird because I’ll start making these little patterns with the boxes, like turning them or setting them in rows that make sense to me, and people are yelling, “Stop, that’s not where that goes.”\n\nAnd sometimes I just kind of freeze there by the forklift with my arms up, like I’m halfway through doing the hand signal, and then I hear the backup beeper and I’ll copy it without really meaning to. That’s what they keep bringing up, I think. My supervisor said it wasn’t even about the boxes being required anymore, it was me doing strange stuff when everyone needed me to move out of the way.\n\nIt reminds me of those crosswalk signals that beep different in different neighborhoods—sorry, I know that’s not what you asked. But yeah, I guess I do get stuck on doing it a specific way, even when it’s not part of the job.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to repeat certain actions or behaviors over and over again?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sort of… I mean, I don’t count like “one, two, three” or anything. But at work they say I keep doing the same kinds of things again, like I’ll get stuck with my arms up by the forklift, like I’m frozen in the middle of a hand signal, and then if the beeper goes off I might copy the sound without really thinking.\n\nAnd the boxes… I’ll move them into these little patterns, or turn them a certain way, even when people are yelling for me to stop because it’s not helping. It’s like my body keeps going back to it.\n\nThe forklift beeping is really loud in that warehouse, though, and it kind of gets in your head. Like the crosswalk noises near the bus stop, they’re all different depending on where you are, and sometimes I notice that more than what I’m supposed to be doing… but yeah, I guess there are things I repeat.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F84.0", "F20.0", "F42.2"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

Like the crosswalk noises near the bus stop, they’re all different depending on where you are, and sometimes I notice that more than what I’m supposed to be doing… but yeah, I guess there are things I repeat.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you believe that the patterns you see have any special powers or abilities?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 20 / laborer_or_freight_stock_or_material_mover / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Special powers? I don’t know… I wouldn’t say powers like, like a movie or something. I just notice them and they seem important in the moment.

Like the way boxes line up, or how the floor tiles make these paths—well, the tiles at the grocery store near my apartment are different from the ones by the bus stop, and that always makes me think about how people decide those layouts. It’s probably just some manager picking whatever’s cheapest, right?

So… no, I don’t think they have powers. I just get caught up in them sometimes.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF84.0, F20.0, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Uh… I guess they brought me in because of work. At the warehouse. People keep saying I’m doing things wrong, but the boxes have a kind of order if you look at them right, like little roads, you know? \n\nSometimes I’ll just stop by the forklift with my arms up, and they yell because it’s in the way, and I copy the beeping sounds or the hand signals the guys do. It’s not really… I don’t know, it just happens. They get mad and tell me to stop moving boxes into patterns when I’m supposed to be loading them.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the patterns you see in the boxes are real, or are they just something you imagine?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, the boxes are real, obviously, you can touch them, and the tape lines and labels, they line up sometimes. But if you mean like, does everybody see it the way I do, I don’t know. \n\nAt the warehouse they just want them stacked fast, like blue pallet over here, damaged ones over there, and I try to explain it’s cleaner if the corners match, like when you’re lining up notebooks for class— I used to do that, keep the edges straight. So… I’m not sure if it’s imagined. It feels like it’s there when I’m looking at it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear voices or sounds when no one else is around?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… sounds, yeah, there’s always sounds, like the building makes noises even when it’s quiet, the lights buzz and the carts kind of squeak in my head after work. Voices though… I mean, if someone’s not there? I don’t really know how you’d tell for sure.\n\nSometimes I think I hear my name, but then it could be the hallway or somebody outside, or like when the forklift backs up and the beep gets stuck in your ears. My old dorm had pipes that sounded like people talking, too, especially at night. So… I haven’t really noticed it in a clear way, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the patterns you see in the boxes have a special meaning or purpose?\n\nPatient: Special meaning… I don’t know if I’d call it special. It’s more like… if the boxes line up wrong, it feels messy, like when a song is off-beat. But purpose, I mean, everything in the warehouse has a purpose, right? Labels, numbers, where stuff goes.\n\nSometimes I think if I put them the right way it makes more sense, but I don’t know that it means anything beyond that. My old notebooks used to be like that too, all the corners even, and if someone borrowed one and bent the pages I’d keep thinking about it. So… I’m not sure. It just feels like there’s a right way they should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you think someone or something is controlling your actions when you arrange the boxes into patterns?\n\nPatient: Controlling… I don’t know. I don’t think of it like someone with a remote or anything. I’m just trying to get them right, like the edges and the labels, because if one is turned weird it kind of pulls my attention.\n\nBut “something” controlling it… I mean, work kind of controls it too, because the scanner tells you where things go, and the supervisor wants it fast, and then you start moving without thinking. Like when I used to walk to class and suddenly you’re there and you don’t remember every step. So… I’m not sure that’s what you mean. I haven’t really noticed someone making me do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you can't stop thinking about arranging the boxes into patterns, even when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know. Sometimes after work I still see the stacks in my head, like when you’ve been staring at something too long and then you close your eyes and it’s still kind of there. But I don’t know if that’s the same as not being able to stop thinking about it.\n\nLike, when I was in school, after doing math homework, I’d keep seeing the numbers for a while, or if a song is playing at the store it gets stuck in your head even if you don’t like it. The boxes are kind of like that maybe. I haven’t really… noticed it in a way where I’d say it takes over or something. It’s just there sometimes, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your way of arranging the boxes or thinking about patterns is causing problems for you in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it is. I mean, I didn’t think it was like a “problem” problem at first, just me trying to do the job right, but then they took me off the forklift. They said it wasn’t safe, or that I was getting too distracted with where things were set, and… I don’t know, I used to be good with the forklift.\n\nAnd school too, I’m behind in my community college classes. I’ll sit down to do the work and then I start thinking about rows and labels and how things should line up, and then it’s like the whole evening is gone. It reminds me of when my uncle would organize his garage by color, but he could still find everything, so I don’t know why mine turns into such a mess.\n\nI also stopped going to the church food-bank shifts. People there were saying they couldn’t count on me, or they didn’t feel like things were safe around me anymore. That one kind of… yeah, that bothered me. I liked helping there. So I guess it’s causing problems, even if I don’t always see it happening right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're thinking about arranging the boxes into patterns even when you don't want to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t sit there and think, “I don’t want to think about boxes,” exactly. It’s more like my mind goes there if something reminds me, like if I see shelves at a store or the way cereal boxes are lined up, and then I start noticing which one is crooked.\n\nBut I don’t know if that counts as even when I don’t want to. Sometimes I’m trying to do school stuff and it pops in, but then I also end up thinking about other things too, like whether I paid my phone bill or what time the bus comes. The bus route near my apartment changed and now the timing is all off, so I have to leave earlier, and that kind of messes up the whole day.\n\nSo… yeah, I guess it happens when I’m not trying to think about it, but I don’t know if I’d say I “don’t want to.” It’s just there sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop thinking about the patterns, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “unable,” exactly. Like, sometimes I try to read for class and then I realize I’m thinking about how the page is laid out, or how the boxes at work would fit better if they were turned the other way, but then… I don’t know, everybody’s mind wanders, right?\n\nIt’s kind of like when my roommate keeps the dishes in the wrong cabinet and then I’ll think about that while I’m supposed to be doing something else. But then I’ll also think about the bus schedule, or whether I left my work gloves in my locker, or that time the labels got put on the wrong pallets and nobody noticed until the end of the shift.\n\nSo… I guess I notice the patterns come up when I’m trying to focus, but I’m not sure if that means I can’t stop. I haven’t really timed it or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong need to arrange the boxes in a specific way, even if it's not required for your job?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it feels like I’m supposed to put them a certain way, even if nobody wrote it on the sheet or anything. But then it gets weird because I’ll start making these little patterns with the boxes, like turning them or setting them in rows that make sense to me, and people are yelling, “Stop, that’s not where that goes.”\n\nAnd sometimes I just kind of freeze there by the forklift with my arms up, like I’m halfway through doing the hand signal, and then I hear the backup beeper and I’ll copy it without really meaning to. That’s what they keep bringing up, I think. My supervisor said it wasn’t even about the boxes being required anymore, it was me doing strange stuff when everyone needed me to move out of the way.\n\nIt reminds me of those crosswalk signals that beep different in different neighborhoods—sorry, I know that’s not what you asked. But yeah, I guess I do get stuck on doing it a specific way, even when it’s not part of the job.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to repeat certain actions or behaviors over and over again?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sort of… I mean, I don’t count like “one, two, three” or anything. But at work they say I keep doing the same kinds of things again, like I’ll get stuck with my arms up by the forklift, like I’m frozen in the middle of a hand signal, and then if the beeper goes off I might copy the sound without really thinking.\n\nAnd the boxes… I’ll move them into these little patterns, or turn them a certain way, even when people are yelling for me to stop because it’s not helping. It’s like my body keeps going back to it.\n\nThe forklift beeping is really loud in that warehouse, though, and it kind of gets in your head. Like the crosswalk noises near the bus stop, they’re all different depending on where you are, and sometimes I notice that more than what I’m supposed to be doing… but yeah, I guess there are things I repeat.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the patterns you see have any special powers or abilities?\n\nPatient: Special powers? It’s probably just some manager picking whatever’s cheapest, right?\n\nSo… no, I don’t think they have powers. I just get caught up in them sometimes.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

I just notice them and they seem important in the moment.\n\nLike the way boxes line up, or how the floor tiles make these paths—well, the tiles at the grocery store near my apartment are different from the ones by the bus stop, and that always makes me think about how people decide those layouts. It’s probably just some manager picking whatever’s cheapest, right?\n\nSo… no, I don’t think they have powers. I just get caught up in them sometimes.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever have thoughts that keep coming into your mind even when you don't want them to?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 13\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
{"key":"None","name":"None","description":"None","reason":"The question is about intrusive, unwanted thoughts, which is not covered by the provided symptom profile items."}

[
  {

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Schizophrenia, in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong need to arrange the boxes in a specific way, even if it's not required for your job?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it feels like I’m supposed to put them a certain way, even if nobody wrote it on the sheet or anything. But then it gets weird because I’ll start making these little patterns with the boxes, like turning them or setting them in rows that make sense to me, and people are yelling, “Stop, that’s not where that goes.”\n\nAnd sometimes I just kind of freeze there by the forklift with my arms up, like I’m halfway through doing the hand signal, and then I hear the backup beeper and I’ll copy it without really meaning to. That’s what they keep bringing up, I think. My supervisor said it wasn’t even about the boxes being required anymore, it was me doing strange stuff when everyone needed me to move out of the way.\n\nIt reminds me of those crosswalk signals that beep different in different neighborhoods—sorry, I know that’s not what you asked. But yeah, I guess I do get stuck on doing it a specific way, even when it’s not part of the job.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to repeat certain actions or behaviors over and over again?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sort of… I mean, I don’t count like “one, two, three” or anything. But at work they say I keep doing the same kinds of things again, like I’ll get stuck with my arms up by the forklift, like I’m frozen in the middle of a hand signal, and then if the beeper goes off I might copy the sound without really thinking.\n\nAnd the boxes… I’ll move them into these little patterns, or turn them a certain way, even when people are yelling for me to stop because it’s not helping. It’s like my body keeps going back to it.\n\nThe forklift beeping is really loud in that warehouse, though, and it kind of gets in your head. Like the crosswalk noises near the bus stop, they’re all different depending on where you are, and sometimes I notice that more than what I’m supposed to be doing… but yeah, I guess there are things I repeat.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the patterns you see have any special powers or abilities?\n\nPatient: Special powers? I don’t know… I wouldn’t say powers like, like a movie or something. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure what counts as that. Like, everybody has stuff pop in their head, right? A song from the radio or remembering you forgot milk or whatever.

Sometimes I’ll get stuck thinking about something, but I don’t know if it’s “unwanted” exactly. Like the sound of the lights in the break room, or the way the carts at the store all pull to one side—there’s this one grocery cart wheel that makes that wobble sound, and then I’m thinking about who fixes those, if anyone even checks them.

Sorry, I’m kind of going off. I guess… I haven’t really noticed thoughts coming in that I’m trying to fight off like that. I’d have to think about what you mean.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF84.0, F20.0, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Uh… I guess they brought me in because of work. At the warehouse. People keep saying I’m doing things wrong, but the boxes have a kind of order if you look at them right, like little roads, you know? \n\nSometimes I’ll just stop by the forklift with my arms up, and they yell because it’s in the way, and I copy the beeping sounds or the hand signals the guys do. It’s not really… I don’t know, it just happens. They get mad and tell me to stop moving boxes into patterns when I’m supposed to be loading them.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the patterns you see in the boxes are real, or are they just something you imagine?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, the boxes are real, obviously, you can touch them, and the tape lines and labels, they line up sometimes. But if you mean like, does everybody see it the way I do, I don’t know. \n\nAt the warehouse they just want them stacked fast, like blue pallet over here, damaged ones over there, and I try to explain it’s cleaner if the corners match, like when you’re lining up notebooks for class— I used to do that, keep the edges straight. So… I’m not sure if it’s imagined. It feels like it’s there when I’m looking at it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear voices or sounds when no one else is around?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… sounds, yeah, there’s always sounds, like the building makes noises even when it’s quiet, the lights buzz and the carts kind of squeak in my head after work. Voices though… I mean, if someone’s not there? I don’t really know how you’d tell for sure.\n\nSometimes I think I hear my name, but then it could be the hallway or somebody outside, or like when the forklift backs up and the beep gets stuck in your ears. My old dorm had pipes that sounded like people talking, too, especially at night. So… I haven’t really noticed it in a clear way, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the patterns you see in the boxes have a special meaning or purpose?\n\nPatient: Special meaning… I don’t know if I’d call it special. It’s more like… if the boxes line up wrong, it feels messy, like when a song is off-beat. But purpose, I mean, everything in the warehouse has a purpose, right? Labels, numbers, where stuff goes.\n\nSometimes I think if I put them the right way it makes more sense, but I don’t know that it means anything beyond that. My old notebooks used to be like that too, all the corners even, and if someone borrowed one and bent the pages I’d keep thinking about it. So… I’m not sure. It just feels like there’s a right way they should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you think someone or something is controlling your actions when you arrange the boxes into patterns?\n\nPatient: Controlling… I don’t know. I don’t think of it like someone with a remote or anything. I’m just trying to get them right, like the edges and the labels, because if one is turned weird it kind of pulls my attention.\n\nBut “something” controlling it… I mean, work kind of controls it too, because the scanner tells you where things go, and the supervisor wants it fast, and then you start moving without thinking. Like when I used to walk to class and suddenly you’re there and you don’t remember every step. So… I’m not sure that’s what you mean. I haven’t really noticed someone making me do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you can't stop thinking about arranging the boxes into patterns, even when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know. Sometimes after work I still see the stacks in my head, like when you’ve been staring at something too long and then you close your eyes and it’s still kind of there. But I don’t know if that’s the same as not being able to stop thinking about it.\n\nLike, when I was in school, after doing math homework, I’d keep seeing the numbers for a while, or if a song is playing at the store it gets stuck in your head even if you don’t like it. The boxes are kind of like that maybe. I haven’t really… noticed it in a way where I’d say it takes over or something. It’s just there sometimes, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your way of arranging the boxes or thinking about patterns is causing problems for you in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it is. I mean, I didn’t think it was like a “problem” problem at first, just me trying to do the job right, but then they took me off the forklift. They said it wasn’t safe, or that I was getting too distracted with where things were set, and… I don’t know, I used to be good with the forklift.\n\nAnd school too, I’m behind in my community college classes. I’ll sit down to do the work and then I start thinking about rows and labels and how things should line up, and then it’s like the whole evening is gone. It reminds me of when my uncle would organize his garage by color, but he could still find everything, so I don’t know why mine turns into such a mess.\n\nI also stopped going to the church food-bank shifts. People there were saying they couldn’t count on me, or they didn’t feel like things were safe around me anymore. That one kind of… yeah, that bothered me. I liked helping there. So I guess it’s causing problems, even if I don’t always see it happening right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're thinking about arranging the boxes into patterns even when you don't want to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t sit there and think, “I don’t want to think about boxes,” exactly. It’s more like my mind goes there if something reminds me, like if I see shelves at a store or the way cereal boxes are lined up, and then I start noticing which one is crooked.\n\nBut I don’t know if that counts as even when I don’t want to. Sometimes I’m trying to do school stuff and it pops in, but then I also end up thinking about other things too, like whether I paid my phone bill or what time the bus comes. The bus route near my apartment changed and now the timing is all off, so I have to leave earlier, and that kind of messes up the whole day.\n\nSo… yeah, I guess it happens when I’m not trying to think about it, but I don’t know if I’d say I “don’t want to.” It’s just there sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop thinking about the patterns, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “unable,” exactly. Like, sometimes I try to read for class and then I realize I’m thinking about how the page is laid out, or how the boxes at work would fit better if they were turned the other way, but then… I don’t know, everybody’s mind wanders, right?\n\nIt’s kind of like when my roommate keeps the dishes in the wrong cabinet and then I’ll think about that while I’m supposed to be doing something else. But then I’ll also think about the bus schedule, or whether I left my work gloves in my locker, or that time the labels got put on the wrong pallets and nobody noticed until the end of the shift.\n\nSo… I guess I notice the patterns come up when I’m trying to focus, but I’m not sure if that means I can’t stop. I haven’t really timed it or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong need to arrange the boxes in a specific way, even if it's not required for your job?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it feels like I’m supposed to put them a certain way, even if nobody wrote it on the sheet or anything. I don’t know if it’s anxiety exactly. I mean, people keep saying I’m messing things up at the warehouse because I’ll move the boxes into these weird little patterns, and then they’re yelling, “Stop, stop,” and I’m still doing it for a second.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Uh… I guess they brought me in because of work. At the warehouse. It’s not really… I don’t know, it just happens. I haven’t really noticed someone making me do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you can't stop thinking about arranging the boxes into patterns, even when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know. Sometimes after work I still see the stacks in my head, like when you’ve been staring at something too long and then you close your eyes and it’s still kind of there. But I don’t know if that’s the same as not being able to stop thinking about it.\n\nLike, when I was in school, after doing math homework, I’d keep seeing the numbers for a while, or if a song is playing at the store it gets stuck in your head even if you don’t like it. The boxes are kind of like that maybe. I haven’t really… noticed it in a way where I’d say it takes over or something. It’s just there sometimes, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your way of arranging the boxes or thinking about patterns is causing problems for you in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it is. I mean, I didn’t think it was like a “problem” problem at first, just me trying to do the job right, but then they took me off the forklift. They said it wasn’t safe, or that I was getting too distracted with where things were set, and… I don’t know, I used to be good with the forklift.\n\nAnd school too, I’m behind in my community college classes. I’ll sit down to do the work and then I start thinking about rows and labels and how things should line up, and then it’s like the whole evening is gone. It reminds me of when my uncle would organize his garage by color, but he could still find everything, so I don’t know why mine turns into such a mess.\n\nI also stopped going to the church food-bank shifts. People there were saying they couldn’t count on me, or they didn’t feel like things were safe around me anymore. That one kind of… yeah, that bothered me. I liked helping there. I mean, I don’t sit there and think, “I don’t want to think about boxes,” exactly. It’s more like my mind goes there if something reminds me, like if I see shelves at a store or the way cereal boxes are lined up, and then I start noticing which one is crooked.\n\nBut I don’t know if that counts as even when I don’t want to. Sometimes I’m trying to do school stuff and it pops in, but then I also end up thinking about other things too, like whether I paid my phone bill or what time the bus comes. The bus route near my apartment changed and now the timing is all off, so I have to leave earlier, and that kind of messes up the whole day.\n\nSo… yeah, I guess it happens when I’m not trying to think about it, but I don’t know if I’d say I “don’t want to.” It’s just there sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop thinking about the patterns, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “unable,” exactly. Like, sometimes I try to read for class and then I realize I’m thinking about how the page is laid out, or how the boxes at work would fit better if they were turned the other way, but then… I don’t know, everybody’s mind wanders, right?\n\nIt’s kind of like when my roommate keeps the dishes in the wrong cabinet and then I’ll think about that while I’m supposed to be doing something else. But then I’ll also think about the bus schedule, or whether I left my work gloves in my locker, or that time the labels got put on the wrong pallets and nobody noticed until the end of the shift.\n\nSo… I guess I notice the patterns come up when I’m trying to focus, but I’m not sure if that means I can’t stop. I haven’t really timed it or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong need to arrange the boxes in a specific way, even if it's not required for your job?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it feels like I’m supposed to put them a certain way, even if nobody wrote it on the sheet or anything. But yeah, I guess I do get stuck on doing it a specific way, even when it’s not part of the job.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to repeat certain actions or behaviors over and over again?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sort of… I mean, I don’t count like “one, two, three” or anything. My old notebooks used to be like that too, all the corners even, and if someone borrowed one and bent the pages I’d keep thinking about it. So… I’m not sure. It just feels like there’s a right way they should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you think someone or something is controlling your actions when you arrange the boxes into patterns?\n\nPatient: Controlling… I don’t know. I don’t think of it like someone with a remote or anything. I’m just trying to get them right, like the edges and the labels, because if one is turned weird it kind of pulls my attention.\n\nBut “something” controlling it… I mean, work kind of controls it too, because the scanner tells you where things go, and the supervisor wants it fast, and then you start moving without thinking. Like when I used to walk to class and suddenly you’re there and you don’t remember every step. So… I’m not sure that’s what you mean. I haven’t really noticed someone making me do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you can't stop thinking about arranging the boxes into patterns, even when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know. Sometimes after work I still see the stacks in my head, like when you’ve been staring at something too long and then you close your eyes and it’s still kind of there. But I don’t know if that’s the same as not being able to stop thinking about it.\n\nLike, when I was in school, after doing math homework, I’d keep seeing the numbers for a while, or if a song is playing at the store it gets stuck in your head even if you don’t like it. The boxes are kind of like that maybe. I haven’t really… noticed it in a way where I’d say it takes over or something. It’s just there sometimes, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your way of arranging the boxes or thinking about patterns is causing problems for you in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it is. I mean, I didn’t think it was like a “problem” problem at first, just me trying to do the job right, but then they took me off the forklift. They said it wasn’t safe, or that I was getting too distracted with where things were set, and… I don’t know, I used to be good with the forklift.\n\nAnd school too, I’m behind in my community college classes. I’ll sit down to do the work and then I start thinking about rows and labels and how things should line up, and then it’s like the whole evening is gone. It reminds me of when my uncle would organize his garage by color, but he could still find everything, so I don’t know why mine turns into such a mess.\n\nI also stopped going to the church food-bank shifts. People there were saying they couldn’t count on me, or they didn’t feel like things were safe around me anymore. That one kind of… yeah, that bothered me. I liked helping there. So I guess it’s causing problems, even if I don’t always see it happening right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're thinking about arranging the boxes into patterns even when you don't want to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t sit there and think, “I don’t want to think about boxes,” exactly. It’s more like my mind goes there if something reminds me, like if I see shelves at a store or the way cereal boxes are lined up, and then I start noticing which one is crooked.\n\nBut I don’t know if that counts as even when I don’t want to. Sometimes I’m trying to do school stuff and it pops in, but then I also end up thinking about other things too, like whether I paid my phone bill or what time the bus comes. The bus route near my apartment changed and now the timing is all off, so I have to leave earlier, and that kind of messes up the whole day.\n\nSo… yeah, I guess it happens when I’m not trying to think about it, but I don’t know if I’d say I “don’t want to.” It’s just there sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop thinking about the patterns, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “unable,” exactly. Like, sometimes I try to read for class and then I realize I’m thinking about how the page is laid out, or how the boxes at work would fit better if they were turned the other way, but then… I don’t know, everybody’s mind wanders, right?\n\nIt’s kind of like when my roommate keeps the dishes in the wrong cabinet and then I’ll think about that while I’m supposed to be doing something else. But then I’ll also think about the bus schedule, or whether I left my work gloves in my locker, or that time the labels got put on the wrong pallets and nobody noticed until the end of the shift.\n\nSo… I guess I notice the patterns come up when I’m trying to focus, but I’m not sure if that means I can’t stop. I haven’t really timed it or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong need to arrange the boxes in a specific way, even if it's not required for your job?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it feels like I’m supposed to put them a certain way, even if nobody wrote it on the sheet or anything. But then it gets weird because I’ll start making these little patterns with the boxes, like turning them or setting them in rows that make sense to me, and people are yelling, “Stop, that’s not where that goes.”\n\nAnd sometimes I just kind of freeze there by the forklift with my arms up, like I’m halfway through doing the hand signal, and then I hear the backup beeper and I’ll copy it without really meaning to. That’s what they keep bringing up, I think. My supervisor said it wasn’t even about the boxes being required anymore, it was me doing strange stuff when everyone needed me to move out of the way.\n\nIt reminds me of those crosswalk signals that beep different in different neighborhoods—sorry, I know that’s not what you asked. But yeah, I guess I do get stuck on doing it a specific way, even when it’s not part of the job.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to repeat certain actions or behaviors over and over again?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sort of… I mean, I don’t count like “one, two, three” or anything. But at work they say I keep doing the same kinds of things again, like I’ll get stuck with my arms up by the forklift, like I’m frozen in the middle of a hand signal, and then if the beeper goes off I might copy the sound without really thinking.\n\nAnd the boxes… I’ll move them into these little patterns, or turn them a certain way, even when people are yelling for me to stop because it’s not helping. It’s like my body keeps going back to it.\n\nThe forklift beeping is really loud in that warehouse, though, and it kind of gets in your head. Like the crosswalk noises near the bus stop, they’re all different depending on where you are, and sometimes I notice that more than what I’m supposed to be doing… but yeah, I guess there are things I repeat.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the patterns you see have any special powers or abilities?\n\nPatient: Special powers? I don’t know… I wouldn’t say powers like, like a movie or something. I just notice them and they seem important in the moment.\n\nLike the way boxes line up, or how the floor tiles make these paths—well, the tiles at the grocery store near my apartment are different from the ones by the bus stop, and that always makes me think about how people decide those layouts. It’s probably just some manager picking whatever’s cheapest, right?\n\nSo… no, I don’t think they have powers. I just get caught up in them sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that keep coming into your mind even when you don't want them to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure what counts as that. Like, everybody has stuff pop in their head, right? A song from the radio or remembering you forgot milk or whatever.\n\nSometimes I’ll get stuck thinking about something, but I don’t know if it’s “unwanted” exactly. Like the sound of the lights in the break room, or the way the carts at the store all pull to one side—there’s this one grocery cart wheel that makes that wobble sound, and then I’m thinking about who fixes those, if anyone even checks them.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of going off. I guess… I haven’t really noticed thoughts coming in that I’m trying to fight off like that. I’d have to think about what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your need to arrange things in a certain way is causing you a lot of stress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s anxiety exactly. I mean, people keep saying I’m messing things up at the warehouse because I’ll move the boxes into these weird little patterns, and then they’re yelling, “Stop, stop,” and I’m still doing it for a second.\n\nIt’s more like I get stuck. Like with the forklift, I’ll have my arms up doing the signal and then just kind of freeze there, and the beeper goes off and I might copy it without meaning to. The beepers are really sharp, though, like they cut through everything, and then everyone looks at you like you did it on purpose.\n\nSo… yeah, it causes problems. Stress, I guess, because people get mad and I don’t always know how to stop right away.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

But then I’ll also think about the bus schedule, or whether I left my work gloves in my locker, or that time the labels got put on the wrong pallets and nobody noticed until the end of the shift.\n\nSo… I guess I notice the patterns come up when I’m trying to focus, but I’m not sure if that means I can’t stop. I haven’t really timed it or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong need to arrange the boxes in a specific way, even if it's not required for your job?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it feels like I’m supposed to put them a certain way, even if nobody wrote it on the sheet or anything. But then it gets weird because I’ll start making these little patterns with the boxes, like turning them or setting them in rows that make sense to me, and people are yelling, “Stop, that’s not where that goes.”\n\nAnd sometimes I just kind of freeze there by the forklift with my arms up, like I’m halfway through doing the hand signal, and then I hear the backup beeper and I’ll copy it without really meaning to. That’s what they keep bringing up, I think. My supervisor said it wasn’t even about the boxes being required anymore, it was me doing strange stuff when everyone needed me to move out of the way.\n\nIt reminds me of those crosswalk signals that beep different in different neighborhoods—sorry, I know that’s not what you asked. But yeah, I guess I do get stuck on doing it a specific way, even when it’s not part of the job.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to repeat certain actions or behaviors over and over again?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sort of… I mean, I don’t count like “one, two, three” or anything. But at work they say I keep doing the same kinds of things again, like I’ll get stuck with my arms up by the forklift, like I’m frozen in the middle of a hand signal, and then if the beeper goes off I might copy the sound without really thinking.\n\nAnd the boxes… I’ll move them into these little patterns, or turn them a certain way, even when people are yelling for me to stop because it’s not helping. It’s like my body keeps going back to it.\n\nThe forklift beeping is really loud in that warehouse, though, and it kind of gets in your head. Like the crosswalk noises near the bus stop, they’re all different depending on where you are, and sometimes I notice that more than what I’m supposed to be doing… but yeah, I guess there are things I repeat.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the patterns you see have any special powers or abilities?\n\nPatient: Special powers? I don’t know… I wouldn’t say powers like, like a movie or something. I just notice them and they seem important in the moment.\n\nLike the way boxes line up, or how the floor tiles make these paths—well, the tiles at the grocery store near my apartment are different from the ones by the bus stop, and that always makes me think about how people decide those layouts. It’s probably just some manager picking whatever’s cheapest, right?\n\nSo… no, I don’t think they have powers. I just get caught up in them sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that keep coming into your mind even when you don't want them to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure what counts as that. Like, everybody has stuff pop in their head, right? Like the sound of the lights in the break room, or the way the carts at the store all pull to one side—there’s this one grocery cart wheel that makes that wobble sound, and then I’m thinking about who fixes those, if anyone even checks them.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of going off. I guess… I haven’t really noticed thoughts coming in that I’m trying to fight off like that. Like with the forklift, I’ll have my arms up doing the signal and then just kind of freeze there, and the beeper goes off and I might copy it without meaning to. The beepers are really sharp, though, like they cut through everything, and then everyone looks at you like you did it on purpose.\n\nSo… yeah, it causes problems. Stress, I guess, because people get mad and I don’t always know how to stop right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your behaviors or thoughts about patterns are affecting your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess it does. Not just friends or family exactly, but people around me. At work they took me off forklift stuff, like I’m not doing those duties anymore, because they said it wasn’t safe or they couldn’t count on me to keep things moving right.\n\nAnd school too—my community college classes, I’m behind now. I keep meaning to catch up, but then the assignments all kind of pile up like boxes, you know, and once they’re stacked wrong it’s hard to… anyway.\n\nI stopped going to the church food-bank shifts too. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know. I mean, related to me like… on purpose? I haven’t really thought of it that way.

Sometimes things line up in a way that catches my attention, like the boxes or the labels or the lights buzzing, and then I get stuck looking at it. But I don’t know if that means it’s about my life. It might just be… things are arranged badly, or too loud, or people leave stuff crooked and then everyone acts like you’re the one making it weird.

Sorry, I’m not answering right. I don’t really know if it’s specifically related to me.
